- **No more yellow dots.** The runes round a Mystic, Infinity or Ahrim's robe's hem were little glowing blocks set round the hips. The skirt sways with your legs and the blocks didn't, so they hung loose under the hem as yellow dots. Now the runes are stitched into the skirt itself, a ring of gold glyphs between two lines of stitching just above the hem, and they fold and swing with the cloth.

## RS-429 - Sconces on posts

- **Sconces instead of standing torches.** The east and front walls of a room are cut low so you can see in, which left nowhere to hang a sconce, so RS-428 lit those sides with standing torches. Now a timber post rises out of the low wall, midway along the east side and near each end of the front, with a torch in an iron sconce on its inner face, matching the ones on the back and west walls.

## RS-428 - Swinging the pickaxe, lit rooms

- **The pickaxe swings again.** Since weapons started being put away out of a fight (RS-422), your arms were set to hang loose after the work pose was set, so mining and woodcutting stood still with the tool in hand. Your arms belong to the work again while you gather.
- **Rooms lit all round.** A room only had torches on its back and west walls, so the side nearest the camera was left in shadow. Now iron torch stands also stand midway along the east side and in the two front corners, wherever nothing else stands there.

## RS-427 - Limp falls, legs that stay on, clear paths, steady lamps

- **Bodies keep their legs.** A two-legged body that fell on its back could end up with its legs folded up behind it, so it looked cut off at the waist. The hip is now a proper joint: a thigh swings forward up to about 120 degrees from straight down the body, and back only about 20.
- **Deaths go limp.** People and four-legged beasts were flung across the room head over heels when they died. Now they take a gentler shove and slump, and their limbs go slack and settle instead of flailing.
- **No altar in the pyramid, and altars are solid.** The burial hall's altar is gone, from floors you've already visited too. Elsewhere you can no longer walk through an altar; you pray standing beside it.
- **Chests don't block the way.** Vaults, cursed chests, locked chests and altars are never placed in a passage, in the neck between two rooms, beside the stairs or by the way out. An opened chest left beside the stairs no longer blocks the landing either.
- **Town lamps no longer flash.** As you walked, the few real lights were handed out again every few steps, so lamps that stayed lit swapped lights and flashed. Now a light stays on its lamp, and only moves when that lamp falls out of range, fading out first.

## RS-426 - The board by the tavern, minions that walk every step

- **The bounty board moves.** It now stands against the tavern's right-hand wall, facing the lane, with the Captain of the Watch on its right.
- **Minions walk every step.** When one of your raised dead is far from its foe, it hurries two squares in a turn. Its figure glided straight to the second square, sometimes cutting across a wall's corner, and when the two steps were on the slant it jumped there outright. Now it walks through each square it steps on, at double pace when it's hurrying.

## RS-425 - The waypoint in line

- **A row north.** The waypoint moves one row north, still centred across the square, so it stands in line with the path that runs from the Peddler's door into the square. The benches move with it.
- **Off the steps.** The estate agent now stands three squares north of the stone instead of right at its steps, and a visiting rare trader no longer sets up on them either.

## RS-424 - Lamps that stay lit, a simpler square

- **The lamps stay lit at night.** A town only has a few real lights, and they go to the lamps nearest you, so the lamps you walked away from went dark. Now every street lamp, door lantern and torch casts a soft pool of light on the ground and glows at its head all night, wherever you are. Where a real light has the lamp, its pool dims so it isn't twice as bright, and a light moving on to a new lamp fades up there instead of snapping on.
- **The waypoint, pared back.** The four obelisks are gone, leaving the three round steps and the runes. The flagstone rings and the kerb round the square are gone too, so the old cobbles are back, with the compass rose still inlaid round the stone.

## RS-423 - The shut tome

- **One book, not two.** When you carried a spell tome shut out of a fight, the open book's pages and boards still showed through the closed one, as if one book were inside the other. When a figure is put together, the small parts of what it carries are joined into one mesh to draw faster, and the open book had been joined onto the tome itself, so it no longer hid when the book shut. Each named part, like the open and the shut book, is now joined on its own, so only the book you're holding shows.

## RS-422 - Cooking on the fire, a new town square, weapons put away

- **See your food cook.** At your campfire an iron grill now stands over the flames. At a hearth, a cast-iron skillet sits on a trivet in front of the fire. What you're cooking lies on it: a trout, a steak, a dragon steak veined with ember, or a bubbling stew. It browns as the bar fills, picks up grill marks and steams, and a fresh one goes on for each you cook.
- **The cooking window steps aside.** While food is cooking, the cooking panel shrinks to a small card in the corner, so you can watch the fire. The full panel comes back when the batch is done. At your campfire you sit on your stool across the flames from the camera while you cook.
- **A proper town square.** The waypoint now stands in the very middle of the square, on three round steps between four obelisks with crystal caps. The square is paved with rings of dressed flagstones round it, with a compass rose inlaid about the dais and a pale kerb edging it all. Four oak benches face the stone, each with a tub of flowers at either end, and there's a lamp at each corner. A portal sets you down just south of the stone.
- **Weapons put away out of a fight.** When you aren't fighting, you put your weapon away. A one-handed weapon hangs at your left hip, and a blade goes in a leather scabbard with brass fittings. A two-handed sword (in its own scabbard), a halberd or spear, a staff or a bow goes across your back. A shield goes on your back, point down, and an off-hand blade on your right hip. When a fight starts, you reach over your shoulder or across to your hip and draw it. Your arms swing free while it's put away.

## RS-421 - Minions follow you

- **Your dead keep up.** With no foe left to fight, your raised dead used to stand where the last fight ended. Now they follow you: they find the way round walls and through doorways to stay within a couple of squares of you, and they hurry when they've fallen behind.

## RS-420 - Minions that find their way, diagonal fighting

- **Minions go round walls.** Your raised dead used to head straight for the nearest foe as if walls weren't there, and got stuck pressing against them. Now they find the way round, through doorways and along passages, to the nearest foe they can actually reach. The townsfolk and guards in a siege do the same.
- **Archers need a clear shot.** A skeleton archer could shoot through walls. Now it only shoots at foes it can see, and moves for a clear line when it has none.
- **Choose your dead.** The Raise button has a small arrow that opens a picker. Choose which of the dead you've learned to raise and which bones to raise it from (Big Bones and Dragon Bones make a stronger minion). Your choice is kept, and "best available" goes back to the strongest you know with the first bones you carry. The button shows which bones it will use.
- **Monsters move and strike on the diagonal.** Monsters now step diagonally when they chase you and strike you from a diagonal square, so they no longer shuffle round to face you square-on. You can strike them diagonally too. No one can strike round the corner of a wall.
- **Auto or manual XP tracking.** The skills menu has an XP tracker switch. On auto, the XP window follows the skills you've just trained. On manual, it shows the skills you tick. Ticking a box switches it to manual.

## RS-419 - Walls, towers, a gate and roofs

- **A town wall worth defending.** In a siege, the wall at your back now stands a man and a half high, capped with merlons, with two gate towers higher still standing out from it. The barred gate between them is a stone gatehouse with an arched passage, two iron-bound oak doors studded along their straps, and a beam across them.
- **Then through the broken gate.** In the streets, the gate you came through stands behind you, broken in: one door hangs open, the other lies torn off its hinges in the street among the splinters, between its towers in the town wall.
- **Houses with roofs.** The streets are lined with stone and timber-framed houses with walls and gabled roofs of red tile or thatch, each house roofed on its own. Like the walls, they turn see-through when they're between you and the camera.
- **Raids are battles too.** When monsters raid a town and you go to defend it, the fight starts in the streets. The watch and the townsfolk are already fighting the raiders, and their leader holds the square. Beat them off for a reward and the town's thanks.

## RS-418 - The siege battle

- **Breaking a siege is a battle now.** When a host besieges a town, choose to break the siege at its gate. The gate is barred behind you, and you and the town's watch hold the field before the walls against three waves of besiegers. Their captain leads the last wave.
- **Then into the city.** When the last wave breaks, the gate gives way and the fight pours into the streets: cobbled lanes between stone and timber-framed houses. Raiders are loose in the lanes, the watch and the townsfolk are already fighting them, and their warlord holds the town square. Whatever was left lying on the field goes into your pack on the way in.
- **The town's people fight beside you.** Guards in the town's plate and townsfolk with whatever came to hand are real figures, ringed in blue, with their life shown over them. Each closes on the nearest raider and strikes it. The raiders turn on whoever is beside them, and a defender can fall. Each is a match for the besiegers they face, and the guards are sturdier. A town with a stronger watch or stone walls sends more guards out with you, and stone walls hold some of the attackers off. You can step past a defender by changing places with them.
- **A bigger reward.** Win in the square and you walk into the saved town to cheers. The reeve pays well, adds a piece from the town's armoury and three guild tokens, and the town's standing with you rises by 5. You can still fall back from the field before the gate gives way; the siege then goes on.

## RS-417 - Doorways as tall as the portal

- **Tall archways.** Every dungeon doorway is now as tall as the way-out portal. Its round stone arch springs from the top of the wall and stands above it the way the portal's does, with a keystone at the crown. The doors are as tall as the arch: straight up to the top of the wall, then rounded to fill it.
- **The treasure room door too.** It stands as tall, with its jambs carried up to the arch. Its straps, lock and ring are spaced over the taller door, and the ring sits at a hand's height.

## RS-416 - Taller archways, smaller torches, a flatter front

- **The watch's torches burn smaller.** The flame on a watchman's torch was as big as a bonfire. It's now the size of a torch in a wall sconce.
- **Higher archways in the walls' own stone.** Dungeon doorway arches now spring from much higher up, so the way through is tall and the arch is a low curve just under the top of the wall. They're built from each floor's own pillar stone instead of a plain grey. The doors are cut to the new arch, so they're taller too, and the treasure room door's straps and lock have moved up to suit.
- **No more tyre round the hips.** Below the belt, women's tunics, jerkins and mail stood out round the hips like a ring. That part of the garment now lies flat across the belly and follows the seat behind, with barely any flare at the hem. The body under it is flatter in front too.

## RS-415 - Archways, a crowned rat king and a proper pyramid

- **Hips that fit the waist.** Women's hips were set wider than men's and their tunics flared out from the belt like a bell, which showed most on dragonborn. Now the hips are set no wider than a man's, and the tunic is gathered closer at the waist.
- **An Egyptian pyramid.** The desert pyramid was a stepped ziggurat. Now it has four smooth faces at the steep slope of the Great Pyramid, cased in pale limestone laid in courses, with a gilded capstone. Its doorway sits under a gabled lintel in the face, and sand drifts against its foot.
- **The rat king wears a crown.** It's bigger now, nearly twice a giant rat's size, with a gold crown set between its ears: five points tipped with pearls, a ruby at the front and a sapphire on either side.
- **Stone archways over every door.** Every dungeon doorway is now an arch of cut stone with a keystone, filled in with stone up to the top of the wall. The door's planks are cut to fit under the arch. Like the walls, the arch turns see-through when it's between you and the camera.
- **A new treasure room door.** The locked door into a treasure room is now a heavy studded oak door bound in iron, with a lock plate and keyhole. It hangs in a stone archway between stone jambs. It used to stand on wooden posts taller than the walls.
- **Real torches in town.** The flames that hung in the air beside the town gates are now torches in iron sconces on the gate towers. The fletchery and the temple have standing torches: an iron basket of tarred wood on a banded pole. The watch carry a better torch, wrapped in leather at the grip and charred at the top.
- **Capes that sit on the shoulders.** Capes used to hang a fixed distance behind the back and float at the shoulders. Now each cape is fitted to the body it's on, armour and all. It drapes over the shoulders and lies on the back, then falls straight from the shoulder blades.

## RS-414 - Long walks stay smooth

- **No more slowdown on long walks.** As you walked, the animals, wandering groups, their rings and the land you'd left behind were taken out of the view but never freed from the graphics card. A three-minute walk piled up more than 500 extra pieces of geometry and about 160 textures, and the land got slower the further you went. Now they're freed when they go: a minute's walk used to leave about 40 behind and now leaves 3.
- **Corpses are cleaned up too.** A fallen creature's model is freed once it has faded away, and so is a roadside fight's when you leave it.
- **Shallows in the right places.** When the land around you was rebuilt, the water could keep the previous area's depth map, so the pale shallows and surf could show up in the wrong spots. The water now always uses the depth map of the land you're on.

## RS-413 - Every creature falls properly

- **Octopuses slump.** The giant octopus, the kraken and the other tentacled horrors used to tip over stiffly, their arms frozen mid-wave. Now the body sags and spreads where it lies, pushed a little by the blow, and the arms writhe slower and slower until they're still.
- **Sharks, frogs and the rest tumble.** Sharks, frogs and toads, treants, the xorn, the roper and the floating eye are now thrown by the killing blow like the other beasts. They roll onto a side, and tall ones fall back like a tree. Their own limbs go limp, and a fish's tail or a frog's legs flop as they die.
- **Swarms scatter.** In a quipper, hyena, scorpion or scarab swarm, each creature tumbles down on its own. A dust devil spins up and blows itself out.
- **No more bent-back legs.** A falling two-legged body's thighs swing back no more than about 20 degrees past the body. They used to fold far behind it at the hips.
- **Corpses lie on the floor.** Many creatures stopped short of the floor and looked frozen, propped on nothing. Scorpions, kalphites and beetles on their backs hung up to 20 cm in the air, and the giant mole, the xorn, treants and the purple worm floated too. Each corpse now rests on the lowest point of its own body, and tumbling ones no longer dip their legs through the floor mid-fall.

## RS-412 - The XP tracker stays where you put it

- **No more jumping tracker.** If you'd dragged the XP tracker somewhere, it leapt up to the top-left corner every time you gained xp (each herb you picked, each log, each hit) and dropped back a moment later. It now stays exactly where you put it. In its usual place under your name it no longer shifts by a couple of pixels either.

## RS-411 - Five fixes from playtesting

- **XP bar wraps round at a level.** When you went up a level, the XP tracker's bar slid backwards from nearly full to the new level's share. It now fills to the end, then runs up again from empty.
- **Open doors stay put.** Some open doors in dungeons vanished and came back as you stepped about, because a door was only drawn while its own tile was in your line of sight. A doorway you've explored now stays drawn like the walls around it.
- **Plain logs mean plain logs.** Recipes that take logs (plain bows and staves, the first arrows and crossbow) showed every kind of log you carried in their count and their "can make" number, though only logs from the level-1 trees can be used. They now count only those. Houses, boats and town projects still take logs of any kind, and now say so.
- **No minimap over the inventory.** In roadside fights and dungeons the minimap stayed on screen over the inventory and other menus. It now goes with the rest of the HUD, as it already did on the land.
- **Smooth world map dragging.** The world map moved a whole map tile at a time while you dragged it, which looked choppy when zoomed in. It now follows the mouse or your finger pixel by pixel.

## RS-410 - Smooth dusk, a real shadow, no more jumping

- **Dusk and dawn without the jump.** Partway through dusk the sun turned from warm orange to blue moonlight in a single frame, and the land lost half its light at once (dawn did the reverse). The moonlight now fades in step with the clock.
- **Your shadow in the dungeons.** Your lantern hangs over one shoulder now, so you cast a soft shadow on the floor to your other side, like the sun's shadow outside. It's redrawn every frame while you walk, so it keeps up with you.
- **No more teleporting.** Tapping a movement key or clicking faster than you walk used to put you two or three tiles ahead of your figure, which then jumped to catch up. Each step now waits until your figure is nearly there, so you move at walking pace and never jump. This applies in dungeons, on the land and in towns.
- **Click-walking through doors.** A click-walk that pushed open a door in a north-south passage stopped there instead of carrying on through. Fixed.

## RS-409 - Open doorways show their doors

- **Open doors are doors.** A doorway generated open now shows its oak door standing open, swung back into the passage, instead of an empty stone arch. This applies to floors you've already visited too.

## RS-408 - Full-height doors, minimap walking in dungeons

- **Doors as tall as the walls.** A dungeon door now fills its doorway from the floor to the top of the walls. The stone lintel is kept only over open arches.
- **Click the minimap to walk.** In a dungeon, clicking a spot you've already seen on the minimap walks you there, whichever way the minimap is turned. Clicking a wall walks you up beside it. Clicking an unexplored spot does nothing, and a click on the minimap no longer reaches the floor drawn underneath it. The town and overworld minimaps already worked this way.

## RS-407 - Rooms, corridors and doors

- **Plain dungeons rebuilt as rooms and corridors.** Rooms are clean rectangles with no stray pillars or ragged cave walls. Corridors are one tile wide through the rock between them, never running along a room's wall or side by side. Every room connects to the rest, with a loop or two. Named sites (castles, towers, temples, graveyards, mines, lairs, pyramids) keep their own layouts.
- **Doorways.** Each room opens through a doorway set between its corner pillars: either an open stone arch, or an oak door on iron straps.
- **Doors work.** A shut door blocks sight and arrows. Pushing it open takes a turn, and it swings back into the passage and stays open, in your save too. Monsters shove doors open on their way through. A click-to-walk path opens a door and carries on through it.
- **The lock protects hardcore characters.** A locked hardcore adventurer now survives death like a standard one (with the usual losses) instead of being deleted. Unlock it to play by hardcore rules again.

## RS-406 - Dungeons stay as you left them until cleared

- **No timed refill.** The RS-405 change that reset a dungeon after a day away is taken out. A dungeon is laid out again only once you defeat its master, as before.
- **Every uncleared dungeon is remembered.** Saves used to keep the floors of only the three most recently visited dungeons, so entering a fourth quietly reset the oldest. That's why dungeons seemed to respawn after time away. Now every dungeon you haven't cleared keeps its rooms, foes and loot. If your browser's storage ever runs out, the save falls back to keeping the three most recent instead of failing.

## RS-405 - Locked characters, dungeons that refill

- **Lock a character.** Each adventurer on the title screen has a lock button. While locked, the delete button is hidden and the character can't be deleted. Tap the lock again to unlock. The lock stays on through saving. A hardcore death still ends a locked character, as that mode's rule.
- **Dungeons refill while you're away.** Return to a dungeon after a full day of the game's clock (about half an hour of play) and it is laid out fresh, with new rooms, foes and loot. Come back sooner and it is as you left it. Your gravestones down there stay, moved onto clear floor. Anything you dropped on the old floors is gone. Floors saved before this patch keep until you next visit.

## RS-404 - New creatures without the hitch, quieter rain

- **New creatures appear smoothly.** A creature kind you haven't seen yet is now sculpted a few parts per frame instead of all at once. It shows up a moment later, without the brief freeze.
- **Rain is 85% quieter.** Every kind of rain is turned down: drizzle, downpour, rain on roofs, on leaves and in caves.
- **Window colours hold steady.** The glass in a room's windows changed colour a few seconds after you walked in. It now keeps the colour of the hour it showed when you entered: rose at dawn, warm by day, deep orange at dusk.
- **Out of the grotto, in front of it.** Leaving a grotto, cave or ruin (won, fled, or turned away at the mouth) now puts you on the tile in front of it, not inside its rock.
- **No light shafts in dungeons.** The beams of light falling from the ceiling are gone. Dungeons are lit by their wall sconces alone.

## RS-403 - The long walk, and the screen's own resolution

- **No more stutter every twenty-odd tiles.** When the land ahead is built, its shaders are now compiled a few per frame before it appears, instead of the whole scene at once in the frame it swaps in.
- **Woods and water build in slices.** The trees, grass, water and mist of the new land were each one long block of work; they now spread over several frames at walking pace.
- **Animals stay put as the land moves.** The deer, rabbits, boars and birds still in reach are kept, and new ones are built a couple per frame, nearest first. All 28 used to be rebuilt in one frame.
- **Creature skins are made ahead of time.** The ten surface finishes (skin, fur, scales, metal and the rest) are made a few rows per frame while the land idles, so the first creature of a kind no longer freezes the game for half a second.
- **Full resolution everywhere.** The open land, towns and dungeons all draw at your screen's own resolution, whatever the quality setting. The dungeon is no longer held to 1,280 pixels across.
- **Downscaling only below 60 fps.** One shared scaler drops the resolution a step only while the frame rate stays under 60, and raises it again after ten steady seconds at 60. Menus and one-off hitches don't count.
- A creature kind you haven't met yet can still cause one short hitch the first time it appears, while its model is sculpted.

## RS-402 - The wild animals

The deer, boar, rabbits and birds out on the land were the biggest cost left after RS-401. Zoomed out at the same Wilderness spot, they took **128 draws** a frame; they now take **59**. The whole frame there went from 751 to about **630** draws (from 1,670 before RS-401), and at the normal zoom from 413 to about **350**.

- **A flock of birds is one draw.** Each of a flock's six birds had two wing meshes of its own, twelve draws a flock. A flock's wings are now one instanced mesh, each wing placed from its bird every frame; and a flock off screen isn't flapped at all.
- **A deer is three draws, not eight.** A wild beast's parts (coat, antlers, hooves, eyes) differed mostly in how glossy they were, so they were skinned as five separate meshes. Seen this small, the gloss doesn't show: their colours go into the vertices and they share one skinned mesh, with only a textured coat or a bending neck kept apart. The beasts that wander the land (encounters, mythic beasts) are built the same way. Townsfolk, your own figure and dungeon monsters, seen up close, keep the careful merge.
- **Test.** `owdraws` now also checks each flock is one instanced mesh, each wild beast at most two skinned meshes, and that the looser merge is switched off again once the land's creatures are built.

## RS-401 - The open land, half the draws

Measured on a player's save in the Wilderness, zoomed all the way out: the land issued about **1,670 draws a frame**, and the fps meter read "3d 22". It now issues about **750**; at the normal zoom, **880 became 410**.

- **Gathering-spot effects batched.** Every gathering spot had its own coloured glow disc on the ground; every fishing spot had two ripples and four bubbles, and every special spot its own glow sprite. Each was a separate draw with its opacity set by hand every frame. A chunk's discs, ripples, bubbles and glows are now one mesh or point cloud each, animated on the card. Chunks are 9 tiles across instead of 6, so there are fewer of them.
- **The land is merged like a town.** Sites, farms, ruins and the land's dressing were hundreds of small separate parts; they're merged by look (RS-348, RS-380), so the land's draws went from about 250 to 45 for the view and from 370 to 70 for the sun's shadow. Anything the game still moves or clicks stays as it was.
- **Creatures off screen aren't animated, and far ones cast no shadow.** A serpent or a many-legged creature rebuilds its body on the CPU every time it moves; these were posed even when out of view. Now only creatures the camera can see are posed, and those more than ten tiles away every other frame. Creatures more than a dozen tiles from you no longer cast shadows, which were a few pixels long but still a draw each.
- **Gathering spots aren't looked over again and again.** The spots round you were looked up tile by tile up to every frame while far chunks were still being built (some 1,800 lookups), and every 0.4 seconds after. They're now re-checked only when something they depend on changes (a step, a turn, the season, nightfall, a spot you've worked), with a 2-second safety net, and the landmark check is skipped for tiles already known.
- **Test.** `owdraws` checks no gathering-spot effect is its own draw, the land is merged, a frame with nothing new looks nothing up, known tiles skip the landmark check, creatures off screen aren't posed, far ones cast no shadow, and the land zoomed out stays under 1,100 draws.

## RS-400 - Dead weight out

- **The skeleton's studio renderer is gone.** From the first-person days, every skeleton was drawn by its own second WebGL renderer into a 900 by 1200 canvas, then copied through three more canvases a frame to make a sprite. In the isometric view skeletons have been real figures in the scene since RS-110, so that path never ran, but the renderer (with its lights and a 2048 shadow map) was still created at boot. Now only the sculpted template is built, and the figures are cloned from it as before.
- **The raycaster's frame buffer is gone.** A half-screen pixel buffer was still allocated for the old first-person view on every resize, and the flee portal still had a painted stand-in for a 3D view that isn't running (it's a model).
- **Test.** `deadcode` checks no studio renderer or sprite path exists, no frame buffer is made, and a skeleton still stands in a dungeon as a built figure.

## RS-399 - The HUD on its own clock

- **The HUD is rebuilt ten times a second, not every frame.** The player plate, hotbar, status chips, side panel, quick bars, buff bar and the clock bar are HTML. Every frame each was rebuilt as a string and compared with the last; that was most of the "hud" time on the fps meter (RS-353). The game loop's own frames now rebuild them every tenth of a second. Anything you do (a step, a blow, a click, a key) still redraws at once, so nothing feels late; what changes on its own, like regen or the clock, shows within a tenth of a second.
- **The minimap and the bar animations are untouched.** The minimap keeps its own clock and turns with the camera between rebuilds; the health and prayer bars still slide and the numbers still count.
- **Test.** `hudthrottle` holds the clock and checks the loop's frames leave the HUD alone inside the window, that an action rebuilds it at once, and that a change on its own shows on the next frame past the window.

## RS-398 - The GPU pass, part five: rain and dust on the card

- **Rain falls in the shader.** The overworld's 900 streaks and a town's 700 were each moved by the CPU and uploaded to the card every frame. Each streak is now laid out once; the card drops it with the clock, wraps it back to the top, slants it in a storm and keeps it round you. Storms are still heavier than rain, and the streaks look the same.
- **Dungeon dust drifts in the shader.** The 180 motes hanging in the lantern light were likewise moved and uploaded every frame. Their drift, the wrap round you and their size now come from the card; a mine's thicker dust still works.
- **Test.** `gpurain` checks that the land rain, the town rain and the dust are shader-driven, that their geometry is never re-uploaded while they run, that the clock and your position reach them, and that a storm turns on every streak.

## RS-397 - The GPU pass, part four: the dungeon's effects in the scene

- **Combat sparks as one point cloud.** Up to 400 sparks from hits, spells and torches were each a 2D circle, projected and drawn over the view every frame. They're one point cloud in the scene now; the CPU still moves them (a few sums each) and the card draws them.
- **Loot beams, vein glows, the hover glow.** A loot beam was two gradients built and filled every frame; it's a shaft sprite and a pool of light on the tile. The glow under every ore vein, cave pool and herb patch looped the whole floor each frame; the glow and its wandering glint are built once with the vein and only animated. The tile under the mouse gets a glowing disc and an outline in the scene; its name is still written in 2D.
- **Arrows and spell bolts fly in the scene.** An arrow is a shaft, a head and a fading trail of air; a spell bolt is a sprite with a wake of fainter ones. Each used five gradients a frame before. The spell's element motes (fire sparks, ice shards, water drops) stay in 2D, as do dragon breath, the thrown missiles, status marks, health bars and hitsplats.
- **The tier-4 spell flash** is in the post shader with the hurt and heal edges (RS-396), not a full-screen gradient fill.
- **Test.** `dgfx` stages a burst, a loot beam, a spell bolt and an arrow with the mouse over a creature, and checks each is in the scene, that no 2D arcs or gradients are drawn for them, that shots land and beams end on their own clocks, and that the flash reaches the shader.

## RS-396 - The GPU pass, part three: the dungeon on its own canvas

- **No more full-screen copy.** The dungeon and roadside fights were drawn into a hidden canvas, then copied onto the game's 2D canvas every frame: a whole-screen, resampled blit, and the browser had to keep the drawing buffer around for it. The 3D view now shows on its own canvas, under the 2D overlay (health bars, hitsplats, the minimap), the way towns and the open land have always worked. The screen shake moves the canvas itself.
- **The frame goes into the post shader.** The dark vignette, the red edge when you're hurt, the green when you're healed, the gold of a blessing and the fade to black were four full-screen gradient fills on the 2D canvas every frame. They're now a few lines in the post-processing shader, drawn in the same pass as the bloom and colour grade. With post-processing off (low graphics), the 2D frame is drawn as before.
- **Test.** `dgcanvas` checks the 3D canvas is never copied, sits under the overlay at the same size, the overlay is clear over it, the shader gets the hurt/heal/glow values, no 2D gradients are drawn for the frame, and the canvas goes away when you leave.

## RS-395 - Smooth shadows on the move

- **Your shadow keeps up with you.** To save work, RS-380 redrew the sun's shadows only every second frame (every third on a phone). A walking figure's shadow was drawn from where it stood a frame or two earlier, so it stuttered along behind you on the overworld and in towns. Shadows are now redrawn every frame while you move, and go back to the cheaper rate when you stand still.
- **Overworld shadows stop crawling.** The overworld's shadow now follows you in whole steps of its own pixel grid, so trees, rocks and buildings no longer shimmer or crawl at their edges as you walk.

## RS-394 - Dungeon minimap turns

- **The dungeon minimap turns with the camera.** It used to stay at whatever angle the town or overworld camera last had, so turning the view in a dungeon left it behind. It now follows the dungeon camera, so up on the minimap is up on the screen.
- **North is marked.** An N on the minimap's rim shows where north lies, as on the town and overworld minimap.
- **Turn buttons.** The two turn buttons now sit on the dungeon minimap's bottom edge too, and go away with it when a menu opens.

## RS-393 - Dungeon sconces

- **3D sconces in dungeons.** Dungeon wall torches used to be painted onto the wall. They are now the same 3D iron sconces as in town rooms: a riveted plate, a curled bracket, a cup and a tarred torch, with a flickering flame. They're scaled to dungeon walls, face out into the room, and hang on plain stone.
- **All torchlight on the GPU.** The walls, floors and creatures were already lit by the graphics card. Leftover code from the old first-person view still rebuilt a light map of the whole floor on the CPU every frame. It covered every tile and every torch at four times the resolution, but was only used to shade the few flat sprites still drawn. That's gone: each sprite now works out its torchlight only where it stands, leaving the CPU more time for everything else.

## RS-392 - Torchlit rooms

- **Wall sconces, not ceiling lanterns.** The lanterns hanging from the ceiling are gone. Every room now has torches in iron sconces on its walls: a riveted back plate, a curled bracket, and a tarred torch with a flickering flame. One hangs on the back wall between the shelves and one on the side wall, kept clear of bookcases and of the hooded figure's dim corner.
- **Natural light indoors.** By day a room is lit mostly by daylight through its windows, and a little less when it rains. At night the daylight is gone and the room is lit only by its torches and fires: darker, warmer, with pools of light around each flame.
- **Torchlight flickers.** The sconces' light now flickers with their flames. The old lanterns gave a steady light.

## RS-391 - The tavern's games, played for real

The games at the tavern used to be a single button and a result. Now you play them.

- **Dice poker.** Five dice each. Click the dice you want to keep, then roll the rest once - or stand. The dealer plays its own hand the same way, keeping its pairs and straights. Hands, best first: five of a kind, four of a kind, full house, high straight, low straight, three of a kind, two pairs, a pair. Win double; a draw gives your stake back.
- **Twenty-one.** A fresh shuffled deck each hand. Hit, stand or double down on your first two cards; the dealer's second card stays face down until you're done, and the dealer draws to seventeen. Aces count one or eleven. A win pays double, twenty-one in two cards two and a half times.
- **The wheel.** Bet red, black or the gold crown and watch the painted wheel spin and slow to a stop under the pointer. Red or black pays double, the crown thirty times.
- **Darts at a real board.** Aim with the pointer and click to throw three darts at a standard dartboard - bull, outer bull, trebles and doubles in their rings. Your hand sways, and steadies as your Ranged level rises. Then the local throws three, aiming for the treble twenty. Beat their total for 20 gold.
- **No walking out mid-hand.** While a stake is on the table, Escape and the close button wait until the hand is played out.

## RS-390 - Dawn and dusk through the windows

- **Window light follows the hour.** Inside any building, the light through the windows is a low rose-gold at dawn, warm white through the day and a deep orange as the sun goes down at dusk, then gone at night. The shafts on the floor, the glass itself and the room's light all take the colour, and it changes as the clock runs - no need to step out and back in.
- **The low sun shows.** Dawn and dusk light is a little stronger in the shafts and the glass than before, so its colour reads against the lamplight.

## RS-389 - A night in a bed

- **You get into bed and sleep.** Renting a bed at the tavern (or sleeping in your own at home) no longer skips to morning in a blink: you climb in and lie down on it, the room fades to dark, and the night passes.
- **You wake to the day.** The room is lit afresh in the morning, so the windows that were dark when you lay down glow with daylight and throw their light across the floor. Then you get up beside the bed, fully rested.
- You can't walk off while you're asleep; the whole night takes about five seconds.

## RS-388 - No gnome hat for the hooded figure

- **The hooded figure keeps his hood.** In a gnome town every townsperson is dressed as a gnome - pointed felt hat included - and the hooded figure was dressed the same way, so he wore a red gnome's hat on top of his hood. Anyone hooded now keeps their hood and their own dark robe in every race's town, taking only the race's face and skin.

## RS-387 - A proper cowl, and room at his table

- **Hoods are cowls, not gnome hats.** A hood's slack used to stand up behind the head in a point; it now slumps softly down the back of the head. Every hooded figure gets it - the tavern's go-between, the cultists, the alchemist.
- **You can stand in front of the hooded figure's table.** When his table was pulled in to his corner (RS-384), the tile it used to stand on stayed blocked; it's open now, so you can walk right up to him.

## RS-386 - Seats, robes, posts, the bank's arrows and where you stand

- **Stools pulled in to their tables.** Every stool round a tavern table now stands right at it, so the regulars (and you) sit at the table with their knees under its edge, instead of a full pace away.
- **Robes drape round the seat.** Sitting on a stool or chair, the robe's sides no longer have the seat poking out through them: the cloth falls over the seat's edges and hangs outside it to the floor. The fold that falls from the knees also hangs clear of the shins, which used to poke through it.
- **Arrows and bolts in the bank.** They kept their metal's colour in your quiver but lost it once banked; now they're coloured in the bank too, with their count. And the quiver rows in the bank's pack list now have a tooltip, like everything else.
- **No more flickering posts.** The wooden posts along the walls inside buildings were exactly as tall as the walls, so their tops fought with the wall top and flickered; they now stop just short of it, and the corner has one post instead of two overlapping. A bed's head posts sit clear of the wall's panelling, which they used to cut through.
- **You stand outside a town's model, not in the middle of it.** On the open land, standing on a town's or a dungeon's tile puts you at the edge of it, on the side you came from, instead of inside the building.

## RS-385 - Gathering no longer halves the frame rate

- **Foraging, chopping and mining dropped the frame rate from 120 to 60.** The hotbar's "stop" slot shows a sweep that fills over each swing, and the time elapsed was written into the bar's own markup, so the whole hotbar was torn down and rebuilt every single frame while you worked. The sweep now carries the moment the swing began, which doesn't change, and the bar is only rebuilt when something really changes (once a swing).
- **The gather bar** over your head only touches the page when a value changes, instead of writing its position, width and text every frame.
- The sweep now lasts a whole swing instead of looping every 1.4 seconds.

## RS-384 - A seat at his table

- **The hooded figure's table is pulled in to him.** It now stands right in front of his corner chair, so he sits at it properly instead of beside a table across the floor. The candle moved with it.

## RS-383 - The hooded figure keeps to the shadows

- **His back to the corner.** The hooded figure now sits right in the tavern's corner by the bookshelf, his back to the two walls, facing out across the room, with his table in front of him.
- **A dim corner.** The tavern's nearer lantern now hangs well clear of his corner, so it sits in shadow; a stub of candle guttering on his table is its only light.

## RS-382 - Walk off a won field; the hooded figure takes a seat; back where you left off

- **A won fight on the open land stays open.** The last foe falling no longer whisks you straight back to the map: the win is paid at once, and you stay on the field to pick up what fell and leave by walking off its edge, as you would to flee. An ambush's sealed edges open as soon as the last foe is down.
- **A portal when there is no way out.** A field you can't walk off (walls all round, an island, a closed-in ruin) opens a portal on the nearest clear tile once the fight is won; step into it to return to the open road.
- **The hooded figure sits down.** The Thieves' Guild's go-between now sits in the tavern's corner chair by the bookshelf, with a table in front of him. Talk to him there as before.
- **You start where you logged out.** A game saved in a town loads in that town instead of on the road outside it.

## RS-381 - The cursor no longer costs frames

- **Moving the mouse dropped the frame rate from 80 to 30** on the open land, in towns and in dungeons alike. A gaming mouse reports its position hundreds of times a second, and every report ran a full pick of what was under the cursor, rebuilding the tooltip each time. A move is now only noted, and the pick runs once a frame at most.
- **The land is no longer raycast triangle by triangle.** The ground under the cursor comes from marching the ray down the heightfield.
- **Creatures are picked by an upright ellipsoid round their figure** (as tall as the creature, a little over half as wide), nearest first, not by their triangles: since RS-379 a skinned body answered a raycast with every one of its tens of thousands. A click beside the figure still finds it by its tile.
- **The dungeon's hover tip** (ore veins and cave mouths) reads the floor tile under the cursor without raycasting the walls, and still asks at most fourteen times a second; a click does the full pick, so wall faces and stairs pick as before.
- **Tooltips follow the cursor once a frame** instead of once an event: each move read the tooltip's size, which forced a layout.

## RS-380 - The GPU pass, part two: towns and the open land merged by make

- **Still props merge by make, not just by material.** Parts of the same kind of material (the same maps, finish, glow and shadow flags) bake into one mesh whatever their colours, which go into the vertices: a stall's painted awnings, a well's trims, a market's crates are one draw each. The town palette's own materials, which the day and night change by hand (the glass's glow), are never swapped for a copy, so the windows still light up at dusk. The chunks of gathering nodes on the open land merge the same way.
- **Window panes merge into their buildings.** A bookkeeping list that nothing ever read kept every pane, sign and banner as its own draw: sixteen panes a building. They now bake into the building with the rest of its glass.
- **Puddles are one instanced mesh** instead of forty-odd little circles, each still its own size and place, filling and drying as before.
- **The sun's shadow map is drawn every other frame** in towns and on the open land (every third on a phone, or when frames come slow): it was a second drawing of every shadow-caster in sight, every frame, and nothing in it moves fast enough to show. The dungeon's torches have had their own clock since RS-323.
- **Measured** on one town built both ways: 1,012 meshes before, 848 after. A frame that draws the shadow map takes about 730 draws and the frames between about 300, where before every frame was a shadow frame of about 750. The remaining draws are mostly the townsfolk (seven or eight meshes each), the doors that swing, the lamps and flames, and one mesh per make per building.

## RS-379 - The GPU pass, part one: every creature as a few skinned meshes

The first stage of the renderer work promised after the model rewrite: fewer draws for the same pictures.

- **Every creature is skinned once, at its template.** Monsters, the beasts of the open land, the townsfolk and your own figure are each drawn as one mesh per make of material (skin, cloth, metal, glossy shell...), with every vertex bound to the joint the animation already moves. A human knight was 84 draws and is 17; a wolf 78, now 9; a bear 44, now 6; a black dragon 149, now 11; a giant spider 87, now 4; a scorpion 92, now 3; a tomb scarab 106, now 9. A copy of a creature doesn't redo the work: it only binds the meshes to its own joints.
- **Spiders, scorpions, crabs and beetles** are skinned too. Their tumbling death takes its box from the skinned body and curls the legs at their joints as before.
- **Weapons and shields baked whole.** A kite shield of planks, rim, boss, rivets and cross was sixty draws; it is two now, inside its own group, so it still swings with the hand and still flies from a dead one.
- **One skeleton per figure**, not one per mesh, so a figure's joints are worked out once a frame and it needs one bone texture instead of seven.
- **Culling kept.** A skinned mesh is still frustum-culled, by one generous sphere round the whole figure, so a creature a torch can't see isn't drawn into that torch's shadow map (six faces each). A corpse thrown by the blow is let off culling, since it can come to rest well outside the standing figure's sphere.
- **Towns load quicker**: the townsfolk were skinned one by one on entering a town; now each kind of figure is skinned once and copied.
- **Measured** in a dungeon room with eight creatures in view (a goblin, wolf, bear, skeleton, dragon, spider, snake and knight), the same room built both ways: 1,159 draws a frame before, 402 after, torch shadows included, for the same triangles (4.5 million to 4.3 million); with the shadows off, 656 draws to 225. A skinned mesh's culling sphere follows its joints each frame (each joint's reach, measured once, round where the joint is now), so a creature is drawn into a torch's shadow map only when the torch can really see it; a sphere round the standing figure, generous enough for any pose, had cost twice the triangles.

## RS-378 - The model and animation rewrite, part three: dragons, serpents, birds and the rest

The last stage of bringing the bestiary's deaths up to the skeleton's standard. Everything that falls now falls as a jointed body.

- **Dragons, wyrmlings, wyverns, hydras, sea serpents, dragon turtles and crocodiles** die as a chain of joints: the body one rigid frame, every neck a chain of its joints out to the muzzle (five for a hydra), the tail a chain to its tip, each leg its bones down to the toe. Thrown by the blow, knocked sideways, skidding to a stop and slumping onto its side; the wings fold down as it falls; the jaws hang slack.
- **Serpents, eels, leeches, worms and the remorhaz** die as a rope: their own spine's points strung on bones that keep their length and bend only so far, the body laid along them. The blow flings the head end; the rest falls where it lies and settles in a wavy line.
- **Birds** tumble out of the air as ragdolls, wings sprawling, neck and legs limp, and lie where they land.
- **A beast knocked past its side rolls back** to lie on it, and a big one propped up by its own necks or legs is turned over as a whole. Nothing goes through the floor or a wall; every bone keeps its length.
- Spiders, scorpions, crabs and beetles keep their tumbling ragdoll of RS-367; oozes, ghosts, swarms and the stranger things keep their own ends.

## RS-377 - The model and animation rewrite, part two: the four-legged beasts

Stage two of bringing every creature up to the skeleton's standard: wolves, dogs, jackals, hellhounds, bears, owlbears, boars, elk, the great cats, rats and moles, and the deer, boar and rabbits of the open land.

- **A back that bends.** The forelegs and the neck now hang from a chest that bends, twists and rolls about the middle of the back, and the sculpted body bends with it, seamlessly (the mesh is bent on the graphics card to follow the chest, rather than cut in two). Every leg has a wrist or a hock, and every paw stands on the ground - a hind paw used to float a little.
- **A real trot.** Diagonal pairs of legs swing together: a leg lifting folds at the elbow or the stifle and tucks its paw, swings forward and reaches out, lands with the paw flat and takes the weight, then drives back under the body and pushes off from its toes. The body rises as each pair drives off and settles as the next lands, the nose dipping a little as it lands; the back rounds as the legs gather and stretches as they reach; the shoulders turn toward the reaching foreleg, against the hips; the head nods against the body's rise and the tail sways with the stride. A hard hit taken makes it flinch and sink.
- **Deaths as a chain of joints.** A killed beast no longer rolls over as one stiff box with kicking legs. It falls as a chain of twenty-seven joints - the pelvis and the chest each a rigid frame, a spine that bends a little, a neck, four legs of three bones, a tail - thrown by the blow, knocked over sideways, skidding to a stop with its feet rather than tripping over them, and slumping the rest of the way onto its side where it lands. Every bone keeps its length, elbows fold back and stifles forward, nothing goes through the floor or a wall.
- **The two-legged chain learned from it too:** a thrown body now skids on the floor with proper friction instead of being dragged to a halt, and a knee or an elbow is bent by moving the joint, not by hauling on the hip or shoulder.
- Dragons, serpents, birds, spiders and the rest come in the next stage.

## RS-376 - The model and animation rewrite, part one: two-legged folk

The first stage of bringing every creature up to the skeleton's standard. This one is for everything that walks on two legs and isn't a skeleton: goblins, orcs, bandits, knights, kobolds, cultists, zombies, dragonborn, the townsfolk and you.

- **A proper rig.** The body now bends and turns at a waist above the belt, while the hips stay with the legs: the torso is two sculpts, chest and pelvis, so a hunch, a twist or a swing no longer drags the whole trunk about as one block. Every leg has an ankle.
- **A real walk.** The pelvis sways over the planted foot, dips as each heel lands and rises as the other leg passes under, hikes the free hip and twists with the stride; the shoulders swing against the hips and the head rides level. Knees fold through the swing and straighten to land, then take a little flex as the weight comes over. Feet lie flat through the stance, push off from the toes and lift clear through the swing. Standing, one foot rests a little ahead of the other.
- **Deaths as a chain of joints.** A killed two-legged creature no longer tips over as one stiff box. It falls as a chain of eighteen joints, thrown by the blow that killed it: every bone keeps its length, knees fold forward and elbows back, nothing passes through the floor or into a wall, and it comes to rest where it lands, in a different heap every time. Its weapon and shield still fly from its hands.
- Skeletons keep their own bones and their own fall. Four-legged beasts, dragons and the rest come in the next stages.

## RS-375 - The puddles, found; menus up top; bigger small folk

- **The town puddles are really gone in dry weather.** The real cause: the town hides whatever is off-screen each frame, and while doing that it switched the puddles back on whenever they were in view. That's why they showed even in a brand-new world. They now appear only in the rain and dry about fifteen seconds after it stops.
- **Menus sit just under the top bar** instead of in the middle of the screen.
- **The dungeon master's card is back** when you reach the deepest floor. The **boss-beaten card** is now a framed panel like the other menus.
- **Kobolds, goblins, gnomes, halflings and imps stand taller.** They're still smaller than you, but about a quarter bigger than before, so you can see them properly.
- **The open treasure room door stays drawn when you stand in its doorway.** It used to vanish while you were on its tile.

## RS-374 - Fixes: puddles, the bank, the bag, the dead

- **Puddles dry up for real.** They used to take a full minute to dry after the rain stopped, so they seemed to stay forever. Now they shrink and vanish in about fifteen seconds.
- **The bank shows your pack and the vault side by side**, in a wider window. On a phone they stack.
- **Arrows and bolts are in the bank's pack list.** Tap a kind to bank the whole stack from your quiver as one bundle. Taking it back out puts it into your quiver again.
- **The Quiver tab is always there.** The bag's tabs now stay on one row; on the All and Resources tabs, their longer counts used to push Quiver onto a second line out of sight.
- **No name plates over citizens.** Point at a townsperson (or tap one) to see who they are. Shopkeepers and the other service folk keep their plates.
- **No boss menu.** Reaching a dungeon's deepest floor shows a notice naming its master instead of a menu in your way.
- **The treasure room door swings open.** Turning the iron key swings the door open on its hinges, with a little bounce, and it stays open. It used to just disappear.
- **No more white corpses.** A creature killed during its white hit flash used to stay white as it fell. The flash now goes with it.

## RS-373 - Framed menus, and fixes

- **Menus are framed panels now.** Shops, the bank, the temple, the loot filter, the vault puzzle, quests, pause, settings and the rest open as a centred panel over the game instead of a dark sheet across the whole screen. Each panel has a gold-edged frame, sizes itself to what it holds, and scrolls if it's long. On a phone it fills the screen with a small margin. The title screen, making a character and a world, death and victory, the equipment page and the waypoint map keep their own full layouts.
- **Menus keep their scroll position.** Banking an item, salvaging or buying no longer jumps the lists back to the top.
- **Arrows and bolts from the bank go into your quiver**, not your pack.
- **Arrows and bolts are only on the Quiver tab.** The All and Resources tabs no longer list them.
- **Puddles dry up.** A spell of rain now lasts two to six minutes, and puddles dry about a minute after it stops. A save that still had rain left over from the old step-driven weather clears as soon as it loads, if the clock says it's dry.
- **Big beasts end up on their side.** Dragons, hydras, crabs and other broad creatures used to rock back upright onto their legs after the death roll. Now they slump the rest of the way over.
- **A locked closet's door is never blocked.** A barrel or crate could stand right in front of it and seal it off; that tile is now always kept clear.

## RS-372 - See what the loot filter will sell; drag ammo from the quiver

- **The loot filter shows what it will salvage.** A new WILL BE SALVAGED list in the menu shows every piece in your bag that matches your settings, with its picture, name, tier and sell price. Hover one to see it in full. Tap **keep** to lock it, and the filter will never touch it. "Salvage them now" sells exactly that list.
- **The Quiver tab works like the rest of the bag.** Each kind of arrow and bolt is a picture with its count. Drag one onto the quiver slot on the figure to load it, or tap it. Hover for its ranged strength and whether it fits your weapon.

## RS-371 - Every creature dies its own way

- **Beasts roll onto their side.** Wolves, bears, big cats, rats, elk and other four-legged creatures topple sideways in the direction the blow pushed them. Their legs kick a few times, then go limp, and the head droops.
- **Folk on two legs are knocked off their feet.** Goblins, ogres, bandits and the like fall back away from the blow, with arms flung out, elbows and knees giving way, and the head lolling. **Their weapon and shield fly from their hands** and land on their own, bouncing and sliding across the floor.
- **Skeletons fall apart.** The skull, ribcage, pelvis and every arm and leg bone clatter down separately into a heap.
- **Oozes slump into a puddle**, and **ghosts rise, swell and thin away to nothing**. Ghosts leave no corpse.
- **The throw matches the blow.** A light finishing tap topples a creature, while a big hit, a critical or overkill throws it much harder (about three times as far). Arrows and bolts knock it flatter and further, fire and lightning throw it up and off to one side, earth slams it down, and poison lets it just slump.
- Nothing goes through the floor or into a wall, as before. Corpses still lie there a few turns, then fade.
- **Chains and braziers on dungeon floors are solid.** Like barrels and crates, they are only placed where they don't block a way through.
- **Opened chests have solid lids.** An open lid used to show the floor through its hollow underside; it now has a proper underside.
- **The loot filter says why pieces are kept.** Under "In your bag now" it lists what holds the rest back: upgrades kept by the "never salvage an upgrade" safety, pieces without any picked bonus, tiers you didn't pick, and protected pieces. With that safety on, anything better than what you wear is kept, and so is anything for an empty slot.

## RS-370 - An ammo tag for the loot filter

- **Ammo is a loot filter tag.** It sits on the BONUSES row with the others and covers arrows, bolts and thrown weapons (darts, knives, chinchompas). Pick **keep pieces with these** to always keep the ammo you find, or **salvage pieces with these** to sell ammo drops for gold and leave everything else alone.

## RS-369 - Solid floors, opening chests, real weather, a proper quiver

- **Dungeon refuse is solid.** Barrels, crates, rubble and bone piles now block you, monsters and click-to-walk. They are only ever placed where they can't cut a way off. Each one is checked against the tiles around it, so no corridor or room gets sealed.
- **Chests are solid and open with a lid.** You bump a chest, or click it from across the room, to open it where it stands. The lid swings up on its hinge with a little bounce and a gleam of treasure, and the opened chest stays. This covers corrupted chests, vaults, locked iron chests and the raid's great chest. A jammed vault stays shut. Mimics still spring when you bump them.
- **No braziers in pyramids.** None are placed, and they're cleared from pyramid floors you've already visited.
- **Wandering groups keep to their own ground.** Land creatures no longer walk out over the sea or rivers, and sea creatures no longer come ashore.
- **Townsfolk get about.** By day they go on errands all over town: to work, the shops, a neighbour's door and the gate. They find their way round the houses instead of bunching on the common. In the evening most go *into* the tavern instead of crowding its door, and they come back out in the morning.
- **Weather runs on the real clock.** Rain, sometimes a storm, comes once every 3 to 7 game days and lasts a few hours. Some mornings start foggy, and the rest of the time the sky is clear. Walking no longer changes the weather, and it turns in town as well.
- **Town puddles dry up.** They fill as the rain comes on, then shrink and fade over about a minute after it stops.
- **House portal: just the map.** The list under the portal map is gone; tap a place on the map to go.
- **Arrows and bolts stack themselves.** Every arrow and bolt you pick up, loot or buy goes into your quiver: onto the loaded stack if it's the same kind, or onto that kind's own stack in the quiver. They no longer pile up as separate bundles in your pack, even when the pack is full. Loose ammo already in your pack moves into the quiver when you load your save. More darts or knives like the ones in your hand join that stack.
- **A Quiver tab in your bag.** It replaces the Salvage tab and lists every kind of arrow and bolt you carry, with the count, ranged strength and which kind is loaded. Tap **load** to switch. The loot filter's salvage button is still under the bag.
- **Runes are sold at the temple.** The alchemist no longer sells them. Temples are in every village and city, the same towns that have an alchemist.

## RS-368 - Maps You Can Zoom

- **The waypoint map zooms and pans.** Use the mouse wheel or a pinch to zoom in on where you point, drag to move around, or use the +, - and fit buttons under the map. Tap or click a town to travel; a drag never counts as a pick.
- **The house portal shows its places on the world map.** Towns, charted islands, dig sites and great landmarks are each marked in their own colour, with a legend, plus your house ("you are here"). Tap one to step through. The list stays underneath. Same zoom and pan as the waypoints.
- Names that would sit on top of each other are left off until you zoom in.
- **Fixed:** the portal's upgrade line said grade 1 adds dig sites. Grade 1 opens the portal to your towns, grade 2 adds islands, grade 3 adds dig sites and landmarks.

## RS-367 - Bugs Die Like Bugs

- **No more corpses half through the floor.** A creature toppled by pivoting on its feet, so the far side of anything wide swung under the floor, and a few turns later the body sank straight down through it. Now the model is lifted as it falls so its lowest point rests on the floor, and when it's time to go it fades away instead of sinking.
- **Spiders, scorpions, crabs and beetles die as ragdolls.** The body is knocked away from your blow, tumbles under gravity, bounces and slides on the floor until it settles, almost always on its back. Its legs go limp, curl in and twitch as the life goes out of them, and a scorpion's tail sags out straight behind it. If it lands the wrong way up, a last spasm rolls it over. It never ends up in a wall.
- This is the first family to get it. Tell me how it looks before it goes to the rest.

## RS-366 - No Edge When Zoomed Out

- **The land around you is built 96 tiles a side** instead of 72. Zoomed all the way out, the camera sees about 26 tiles from you on a 16:9 screen (31 on an ultrawide), and the old land wasn't rebuilt until you were 20 tiles from its middle, so you could see past its edge to the coarse far ground, and then the real land popped in.
- **The rebuild follows your zoom.** Zoomed out, it starts early enough that the edge never comes into view; zoomed in, it waits longer, so it happens less often than before. If you run toward the edge while it's building, it finishes at once.
- **Cost:** in a test world, building the bigger land takes about a quarter longer (spread over frames as before), and a fully zoomed-out frame draws about a tenth more.

## RS-365 - Phone Tier on a Computer

- **New setting: Settings, Graphics, phone tier.** It gives a computer the lighter graphics a phone gets: four town lamps lighting the scene instead of eight, plain shadow edges, smaller shadow maps (the sun's 1024 instead of 2048, each dungeon light's 256 instead of 512), no anti-aliasing, and at most 1.5x pixels. It switches there and then, with no reload, and switching it off brings everything back.
- **Why:** a phone in town can outrun a desktop, partly because it runs this tier and partly because a recent phone chip is quicker on a single core, which is where the game's JavaScript runs. This lets you see how much of the difference is the graphics.
- On a phone or tablet the tier is always on, as before.

## RS-364 - Loot Filter Bonuses

- **The loot filter picks by the bonuses gear really has:** melee attack, strength, defence, ranged, magic, spell damage and prayer. RS-362 listed the old affixes (strength, dexterity, wisdom, life...), which no drop carries any more, so the stat picks did nothing. Now "keep pieces with ranged and magic" salvages the melee-only gear, and "salvage pieces with melee attack" takes swords and staves but not bows (a bow's accuracy counts as ranged).
- **The quality row is gone.** On a drop, Normal, Magic and Rare are only the colour of its tier, so the tier row already covers it.

## RS-363 - Dungeons Run Like RS-347 Again

Dungeons felt smoother in RS-347. Three changes since then made the difference, and this puts them back without losing the fixes in between.

- **Fixed a bug from RS-361.** The new shadow timing kept its frame-time average in the same place as the dungeon's resolution scaler. That average then moved twice a frame and lost its resets, so the resolution changed more jumpily than it should. The shadows now keep their own average.
- **Shadows back to RS-347's pace, spread evenly.** Above 45 fps every light's shadow is drawn every second frame, the same work as RS-347, with the torches staggered so half go on each frame instead of all at once. RS-361 had drawn the lantern's every frame, about a fifth more work. Below 45 fps the slower clocks still apply.
- **The picture can drop to 60% in a heavy fight** on a desktop (70% since RS-355; RS-347 went to 50%). It comes back up when the fight eases.

## RS-362 - Pick Your Salvage

- **The loot filter is a menu now.** The bag's "loot filter" button opens it. Pick the **tiers** to salvage (by the level a piece needs to wear, so tier 3 is mithril, studded leather, splitbark and willow alike), the **qualities** (normal, magic, rare) and the **stats**. For stats you choose whether a piece with them is *kept* (salvage everything else) or the only kind *salvaged*.
- **Salvaging pays the sell price in gold.** Turn the filter on and a matching drop is salvaged the moment it drops. "Salvage them now" in the menu, the bag's salvage button or **J** sweeps your bag. The menu shows how many pieces in your bag match and what they'd fetch.
- **Never touched:** sets, Uniques, relics, god staves and capes, superior and quest gear, locked pieces, potions and scrolls. "Never salvage an upgrade" (on by default) keeps anything better than what you wear.
- **The junk tag is gone.** No more greyed-out JUNK pieces, "junk to you", Junk tab or "sell all junk". The bag's last tab is now **Salvage**, showing what your filter would take. The fence buys what your filter picks, still at +30%. The filter starts off; set it up once in the menu (it's saved with your character).
- **Sacred crocodiles are great beasts,** about two tiles nose to tail, twice a giant crocodile. They were smaller than one.

## RS-361 - Snakes keep their shape, shadows keep up

- **Fixed snakes stretching across the room.** Since RS-346 a serpent's slither added its wave to the coil's points in place every frame it moved, and those points are kept between frames, so a snake that kept moving had its coil pulled out longer and longer, until the tube ran right across a pyramid's hall and through your feet. The wave now goes on a copy. A test slithers a snake for four hundred frames and checks it stays where it was.
- **Serpents sized by footprint.** A snake is long and low, so sizing it to a tall creature's height could blow a giant one up; a snake now covers about a tile, a giant one two, a mythic serpent three.
- **Dungeon shadows at full rate when the machine can.** RS-355 put each light's shadow on its own clock (the lantern every second frame, each torch every sixth), which read as a stutter in the shadows at 60 fps. The clocks now follow the frame rate: at a healthy rate the lantern's shadow is drawn every frame and each torch's every other; when the frame rate drops, the slower clocks come back.

## RS-360 - The Black Spire stands

- **The Black Spire is a landmark of its own.** A tower of black glass, eighteen tiles high, eight-sided and ribbed, with knife-edged buttresses at its foot, violet slit windows, a sealed gate and a cold violet flame at its crown trailing black smoke. It stands on clear ground as far from where you began as the land allows, clear of the Wilderness, and shows on the world map with its own glyph and name. Existing worlds get it too: no new world needed.
- **Only the quest opens it.** Walk into it or tap it. Before the Waking Lich quests reach it, the gate is sealed and the Spire only tells you something inside is dreaming. On "Go to the Black Spire" the gate dialog, the knight's floor and the six-floor climb to the Wakener follow in order; mid-quest it offers the climb again. The Spire is a necro tower in its own right, with its own floors remembered between visits.
- **Afterwards it's yours.** Once the Wakener is dust, tap the Spire whenever you like: eight floors of the dead keep rising on its stairs, and every kill in there counts twice toward a Slayer task.
- **A quest that was already pointed at an old tower** (from before this release) is pointed at the Spire when you load.

## RS-359 - The Black Spire's gate

- **The Waking Lich VIII can be finished.** Stepping onto the Black Spire's tile opened the necro tower's plain "descend" prompt, which swallowed the quest's arrival: the knight's floor never showed, and the tower you entered was an ordinary one. Now any quest that led you to a site speaks first when you arrive. At the Spire that means the gate dialog, the knight's floor, and then the six-floor climb to the Wakener.
- **Other quest sites too.** A graveyard watch at night, a castle, a mine or a dungeon a quest sent you to now opens the quest's own dialog on arrival instead of the dungeon prompt. By day, a night-only watch still shows the prompt as before.
- **If you leave the Spire part way,** walking back in through the tower's prompt is fine: the tower stays six floors deep with the Wakener at the top until the quest is done.

## RS-358 - Diag crash fix

- **Fixed a crash when the fps diag ran on a real graphics card** ("Cannot create property 'sprites' on number"). The new per-step store in RS-357 shared a name with the card timer's running total, which only exists where there is a hardware timer, so the tests in the software renderer never hit it.

## RS-357 - A diag you can trust

- **The fps diag reads a fresh base before every step.** It now goes base, sprites, base, grass, base, lamps and so on, and each saving is measured against the base read just before it. The old single base at the start drifted as the card's clocks and the scene changed, so later steps read nonsense (two steps that switch nothing off claimed to save 6 ms).
- **It keeps averaging while the meter is up.** Leave it running through two or three cycles and the readings settle; the line shows `x2`, `x3` for the number of cycles so far.
- **The meter's second line says whether shadows are on**: `sh:off`, `sh:pcf` or `sh:soft` after the buffer size, so a reading can be matched to the graphics setting it was taken on.

## RS-356 - Townsfolk as skinned meshes

- **Each townsperson is now a handful of draws instead of about thirty.** Villagers and town NPCs are rebuilt into GPU-skinned meshes: every part of the same make (same material, texture, shininess and shadow setting) is sewn into one mesh whose vertices follow the existing joints. A person who was 28 to 40 separate parts is now 8 to 11, and in a full city the people draw about a third as many meshes as before.
- **They still move exactly as before.** Walking, striking, sitting, turning the head and hit flashes all run on the same joints; only the way the GPU is handed the triangles changed. Their colours are baked into the vertices, so the figures look the same.
- **Your own figure is untouched**, so outfit changes and equipment swaps keep working the same way.
- **Why:** meter readings on a Windows PC with a strong card showed the frame in a city was spent issuing draws on the CPU (3d 13 ms against 4 ms of GPU work on Low), not on pixels. Fewer draws per person is the lever that moves that number.

## RS-355 - The Processor's Share

- **What the readings said.** On a GeForce 3080 Ti the card's time matched the processor's time to the millisecond, in a town at 2560x1440 and in a dungeon at 640x299 alike. A card isn't slow at the same speed at both sizes: it was being fed slowly. The frame's cost is the processor walking every object in the scene and issuing its draws, not pixels. In a dungeon the biggest part was the six torch and lantern shadows, each of which is six faces of the whole floor, redrawn every other frame - and more every creature that joined the fight.
- **Dungeon lights on their own clocks.** The lantern's shadow refreshes every second frame, each torch's every sixth, staggered, instead of all six every other frame: about a quarter of the shadow work.
- **Towns skip whole things the camera can't see.** A building, a tree or a person outside the view is skipped with every part under it, instead of each of its parts being tested and walked. A generous margin keeps a tall thing just off screen casting its shadow into view.
- **A desktop dungeon stays sharp.** The dungeon view lowers its render scale when frames run slow, an old measure that assumes the card is the limit. On a desktop it now never drops below 0.7; it was going down to half, and the floor went soft for nothing.
- **The diagnosis, on your own machine.** Double-click the fps meter and it switches one thing off at a time for a second each - sprites, grass, lamps, soft shadows, shadows, the multisampled post buffer, people, the ground, see-through parts - and reads how many milliseconds each one costs, as a third line. Double-click again to stop; everything is put back.
- **The far ground stays out of the real land.** RS-349's far ground poked up through the real land where its coarse mountains and sea floor stood higher than the real land's dips: white slabs on the mountainsides, a patch of water on the shore. It's now cut out entirely inside the built land, and the cut-out moves with it.
- **Tests.** `cull` for the first three changes, `diag` for the diagnosis, `landfar` for the cut-out.

## RS-354 - Read the Card

- **The fps meter splits the card's time.** The "gpu" figure now reads, say, "gpu 15.2 (sh 3.0 sc 10.1 post 2.1)": the sun's shadow pass, the scene itself, and the post-processing passes. The line ends with the size of the picture being drawn, like "@2560x1440", since a display scaled up in Windows draws more pixels than its screen has. A reading of the RS-353 meter on a GeForce 3080 Ti put 15 of a 20 millisecond frame on the card in a city, far more than its triangles explain, and this says which pass it is.

## RS-353 - Read the Frame

- **The flicker on your figure is gone.** RS-352 drew the sun's shadow map every other frame. A walking figure then stood in the shadow of its own last pose on alternate frames, which flickered on the figure itself. The shadow map is drawn every frame again. The townsfolk's merged parts stay.
- **The fps meter says where a frame's time goes.** Under the fps and the card's name it now reads a second line, say "3d 4.1 hud 9.8 loop 1.2 gpu 3.0 ms": the time to submit the 3D view's draws, the HUD and interface work round it, the rest of the frame, and the card's own drawing time where the browser can report it. Nothing is measured while the meter is off. If a town runs slow on a strong machine, these four numbers say which part to cut, instead of guessing.
- **Test.** `profile` checks the timings nest (the view inside the game render inside the loop), that nothing is measured with the meter off, and that the meter reads the split.

## RS-352 - Half the Draws Again

- **The sun's shadow map is redrawn every other frame.** In towns and on the open land the shadow pass was over half of a frame's draws: 812 of 1,310 in a town. It's now drawn on alternate frames, which no eye can tell, with a fresh scene drawing it at once. A town frame now alternates between about 470 and 1,210 draws instead of 1,310 every time - about 840 on average.
- **The townsfolk draw a third fewer parts.** Each townsperson was forty-odd meshes. The ones under one joint that share a material - a boot and its sole, a sleeve and its cuff - are now baked into one, so the figure still bends at every joint but draws 30 percent fewer parts. Anything the rig moves on its own, a jaw, an eye, a weapon, stays its own. Your own figure is untouched.
- **Why this matters on a strong PC.** With the right card confirmed (RS-351), 50 frames a second in a town on a GeForce 3080 Ti meant the limit was the processor issuing draws, not the card drawing pixels: a browser draws everything from one thread, at roughly a 60th of a millisecond a draw. Fewer draws is the only way up from there.
- **Test.** `jointmerge` checks the townsfolk draw at least a quarter fewer parts and still walk and strike, that your figure is untouched, and that the shadow map alternates.

## RS-351 - The Fast Card

- **Towns and the open land ask for the fast graphics card.** On a machine with two cards - a desktop with an onboard Intel chip still switched on beside its GeForce, or a laptop - the browser may hand a page the slow one. The dungeon view already asked for the high-performance card; the town and the land didn't, so they could end up drawn by the onboard chip while the dungeon ran on the GeForce. Both ask now. If you've had towns running badly on a strong PC, this is the likeliest cause.
- **The fps meter names the card.** Turn the fps meter on in Settings and it reads, say, "63 fps - NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3080 Ti", for whichever view you're in. If it names an Intel UHD or a Radeon Graphics chip on a machine that has a better card, Windows is giving the browser the wrong one: Settings, System, Display, Graphics, add your browser and set it to High performance.
